methods.txt\r\nFrom \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\"\r\n\r\nOur goal is to decode behavior y \\in R^P from spike-sorted, temporally binned spike counts. For each trial k, neural activity has size N x T, where N is the num\r\nber of neurons and T the number of time steps. Recordings are split into 2-s trials, each divided into 20-ms bins, producing T = 100 time steps. For each trial,\r\n we constructed X \\in R^{N x T}  by aggregating spike counts and used it to compute a decoder estimate of the true behavior y \\in R^P. y is constant within a tr\r\nial (“per-trial decoding”); when P = T, y varies across time (“per-timestep decoding”).\r\n\r\nWe apply our models to 433 IBL sessions,17 covering 270 brain regions and four behavioral variables: choice, prior, wheel speed, and whisker motion energy.\r\n\r\n In the IBL experiments, mice rotate a wheel to indicate the location of a visual stimulus, defined as their choice (Figure 1A). For the first 90 trials, the st\r\nimulus appears randomly on either side with equal probability; in subsequent trials, it appears predominantly on one side in blocks. Mice adapt their behavior t\r\no these changing probabilities, allowing estimation of each mouse’s trial-by-trial prior belief (prior) about stimulus location. Additionally, wheel speed and w\r\nhisker motion energy near the whisker pad are recorded. Whisker motion energy is computed as the mean absolute difference between adjacent video frames within a\r\n bounding box anchored between nose tip and eye. Choice and prior are static within a trial, while wheel speed and whisker motion energy are time-varying signal\r\ns sampled at 60 Hz. See STAR Methods sections “data processing” and “hyperparameter selection” for details.\r\n\r\nData processing\r\n\r\nIBL trial-aligned dataset\r\n\r\nFor choice, we align trials to the stimulus onset, considering neural activity from 0.5 s before to 1.5 s post-onset. For prior, we also align trials to the sti\r\nmulus onset, including neural activity from 0.6 s to 0.1 s pre-onset. The prior represents the mice’s estimate of the stimulus side probability. We use the same\r\n decoding window as in the previous study,27 focusing on the period with minimal wheel movements. Within each trial, we segment neural activity into 50-ms non-o\r\nverlapping time bins. For each time bin, we bin spike counts using all neurons, sorted by Kilosort 2.5,77 from each session. For dynamic behaviors (wheel speed\r\nand whisker motion energy), we select the first movement onset as the alignment event, and decode the behavior starting at the alignment event and ending at 1 s\r\n after the alignment event. The neural activity within each trial is binned into non-overlapping 20 ms bins. For each time bin, we similarly bin spike counts us\r\ning all neurons from each session.\r\n\r\nIBL trial-unaligned dataset\r\n\r\nWe focus only on wheel speed and whisker motion energy, as variables like choice and prior are only defined during experimental trials, which are periods when s\r\ntimuli are presented and mice respond for rewards. Unlike the trial-aligned dataset, we do not align neural activity and behavior to a specific event onset. Ins\r\ntead, we segment the continuous Neuropixels recordings into 2-s snippets to construct the trial-unaligned dataset for each session. These snippets cover interva\r\nls both during and outside experimental trials, capturing the repetitive, structured behaviors within trials as well as the more spontaneous behaviors outside o\r\nf trials.\r\n\r\nFrom \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\"\r\n\r\nWe trained 139 mice (94 male and 45 female) on the International Brain Laboratory (IBL) decision-making task (Fig. 1a,b). On each trial, a visual stimulus appea\r\nred to the left or right on a screen, and the mouse had to move it to the centre by turning a wheel with its front paws within 60 s (Fig. 1c). After an initial\r\n90 unbiased trials, the prior probability for the stimulus to appear on the left or right side was constant over a block of trials at a ratio of 20:80% (right b\r\nlock) or 80:20% (left block). Blocks lasted for between 20 and 100 trials, which were drawn from a truncated geometric distribution (empirical mean of 51 trials\r\n). Block changes were not cued. Stimulus contrast was uniformly sampled from 5 possible values (100, 25, 12.5, 6.25 and 0%). The 0% contrast trials, when no sti\r\nmulus was presented, were assigned to a left or right side following the probability distribution of the block. This allowed mice to perform above chance by inc\r\norporating this prior in their choices. Following a wheel turn, mice received positive feedback in the form of a water reward, or negative feedback in the form\r\nof a white-noise pulse and a 2-s time out. The next trial began after a delay, followed by a quiescence period during which the mice had to hold the wheel still\r\n.\r\n\r\nRecordings\r\n\r\n[... output limited to 10000 bytes; 69 interior bytes omitted ...]\nwhich typically represent the ipsilateral side (Fig. 2a). Recordings were collected by 12 laboratories in Europe and the USA, with most recordings using 2 si\r\nmultaneous probe insertions. To ensure reproducibility, one brain location was targeted in every mouse in every laboratory, as described elsewhere25. Only sessi\r\nons with at least 400 trials were retained for further analyses. Data were uploaded to a central server, preprocessed and shared through a standardized interfac\r\ne. To perform spike sorting on the recordings, we used a version of Kilosort with custom additions. This process produced 621,733 units (including multineuron a\r\nctivity), averaging 889 per probe. To separate individual neurons from clusters of multineuron activity, we then applied stringent quality-control metrics (base\r\nd on those described in ref. 15), which identified 75,708 well-isolated neurons, averaging 108 per probe.\r\n\r\nMethods\r\n\r\nn brief, at the beginning of each trial, the mouse was required to not move the wheel for a quiescence period of 400–700 ms. After the quiescence period, a visu\r\nal stimulus (Gabor patch) appeared on either the left or right (±35° azimuth) of the screen, with a contrast randomly sampled from a predefined set (100, 25, 12\r\n.5, 6 or 0%). A 100-ms tone (5-kHz sine wave) was played at stimulus onset. Mice had 60 s to move the wheel and make a response. Stimuli were yoked to the rotat\r\nion of the response wheel, such that a 1-mm movement of the wheel moved the stimulus by 4 visual degrees. A response was registered if the centre of the stimulu\r\ns crossed the  ±35° azimuth line from its original position. If the mouse correctly moved the stimulus 35° to the centre of the screen, it immediately received\r\na 3-μl reward; if it incorrectly moved the stimulus 35° away from the centre, it received a time out. If the mouse responded incorrectly or failed to reach eith\r\ner threshold within the 60-s window, a white-noise burst was played for 500 ms and the inter-trial interval was between 1 and 1.5 s. In trials for which the vis\r\nual stimulus contrast was set to 0%, the mouse had to respond as for any other trial by turning the wheel in the correct direction (assigned according to the st\r\natistics of the prevailing block) to receive a reward, but the mouse was not able to perceive whether the stimulus was presented on the left or right side of th\r\ne screen. The mouse also received feedback (noise burst or reward) on 0% contrast trials.\r\n\r\nEach session started with 90 trials in which the probability of a visual stimulus appearing on the left or right side was equal. Specifically, the 100%, 25%, 12\r\n.5% and 6% contrast trials were each presented 10 times on each side, and the 0% contrast was presented 10 times in total (that is, the ratio of the 100, 25, 12\r\n.5, 6 and 0% contrasts were set at 2, 2, 2, 2 and 1, respectively). The side (and thus correct movement) for the 0% contrast trials was chosen randomly between\r\nthe right and left with equal probability. This initial block of 90 trials is referred to as the unbiased block (50:50).\r\n\r\nAfter the unbiased block, trials were presented in biased blocks: in right-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 80% of the trials, whereas in left-bias\r\n blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 20% of the trials. The ratio of the contrasts remained as above (2:2:2:2:1). Whether the first biased block in a sessi\r\non was left or right was randomly chosen, and blocks were then alternated. The length of a block was drawn from an exponential distribution with scale parameter\r\n of 60 trials, but truncated to lie between 20 and 100 trials.\r\n\r\nThe automated shaping protocol for training involved two collections of sessions. In the first session, the animals started performing a version of the task wit\r\nhout biased blocks and were then progressively introduced to harder stimuli with weaker contrasts as they became progressively more competent. They also experie\r\nnced a debiasing protocol, which was intended to dissuade them from persisting with just one of the choices. Once they were performing sufficiently well on all\r\nnon-zero contrasts, they were faced with the biased blocks. When, in turn, performance on those was adequate (including on 0% contrast trials, which are informe\r\nd by the block), they graduated to recording. Supplementary Figure 8a shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the first and second co\r\nllections (these were not correlated). Supplementary Figure 8b shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the second collection and the\r\nperformance during the recording sessions. These were also not correlated.\r\n\r\nInclusion criteria\r\n\r\nWe applied a set of inclusion criteria to sessions, probes and neurons to ensure data quality.
